The Multiplication Formulas of Weighted Quantum Cluster Functions
Zhimin CHEN a, Jie XIAO b and Fan XU c

Abstract
By applying the property of Ext-symmetry and the affine space structure of certain fibers, we introduce the notion of weighted quantum cluster functions and prove their multiplication formulas associated to abelian categories with Ext \operatorname{Ext} -symmetry and 2-Calabi–Yau triangulated categories with cluster-tilting objects.



keywords
weighted quantum cluster functions; cluster categories; 2-Calabi–Yau triangulated categories; preprojective algebras

AMS
17B37; 16G20; 17B20

1
Introduction, notation and main results

Cluster algebras were introduced by Fomin and Zelevinsky
[
9
]
in order to find an algebraic framework for understanding total positivity in algebraic groups and canonical bases in quantum groups. They are commutative algebras over
\mathbb{Z}
generated by certain elements called cluster variables. There is an iterative procedure called mutation to obtain new cluster variables from initial cluster variables. For an cluster algebra of finite type, the cluster variables are in bijection with the almost positive roots of the corresponding simple Lie algebra.

Cluster algebras have close connections to representation theory of algebras via cluster categories and cluster characters. The cluster category was constructed by Buan–Marsh–Reineke–Reiten–Todorov
[
3
]
as a quotient of the bounded derived category of the module category of a finite-dimensional hereditary algebra. Given an acyclic quiver
Q
, the cluster category
\mathcal{C}_{Q}
associated with
Q
is the orbit category
\mathcal{D}^{b}(Q)/\tau\circ[-1].
Indecomposable objects in
\mathcal{C}_{Q}
correspond to the almost positive roots in the root system of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{Q}
of type
Q
. The cluster category has the 2-Calabi–Yau property, i.e.,

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{Q}}(M,N[1])\cong D\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{Q}}(N,M[1])

for any
M,N\in\mathcal{C}_{Q}
.

For a Dynkin quiver
Q
with
n
vertices, Caldero and Chapoton
[
4
]
defined a map
X_{?}
from the set of objects in the cluster category
\mathcal{C}_{Q}
to
\mathbb{Q}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
such that

X_{M}=\sum_{\underline{e}}\chi(\mathrm{Gr}_{\underline{e}}(M))x^{-B\underline{e}-(I_{n}-R)\underline{m}}

for a
\mathbb{C}Q
-module
M
and
X_{P[1]}=x^{\underline{\operatorname{dim}}P/\mathrm{rad}P}
for a projective
\mathbb{C}Q
-module
P
(See
[
6
]
for the notation in the above formula.)
In particular, it gives a bijection between the indecomposable rigid objects and cluster variables and then the mutation rule of cluster variables is written as the following property. For indecomposable rigid objects
M
,
N
in
\mathcal{C}_{Q}
with
{\operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{Q}}(M,N[1])=1}
, we have
X_{M}\cdot X_{N}=X_{L}+X_{L^{\prime}}
, where
L
,
L^{\prime}
are the middle terms of two non-split triangles induced by
\varepsilon\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{Q}}(M,N[1])
and
\varepsilon^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{Q}}(N,M[1])
, respectively. This map is called the cluster character. Then the cluster character
X_{M}
for an indecomposable rigid object
M
can be viewed as the general formula of a cluster variable, computed from the initial variables
X_{P_{1}[1]},\dots,X_{P_{n}[1]}
using the mutation rule. Caldero and Keller
[
5
]
generalized these results to any acyclic quiver and then categorified all cluster algebras with acyclic initial clusters. As a corollary, the cluster algebra
\mathcal{A}(Q)
is generated by
X_{M}
for all rigid objects
M\in\mathcal{C}_{Q}
. Palu
[
16
]
generalized the definition of cluster character to a 2-Calabi–Yau triangulated category
\mathcal{C}
with a cluster tilting object
T
. His cluster character, denoted by
X^{T}_{?}
, is given by the formula

X^{T}_{M}=\sum_{\underline{e}}\chi(\mathrm{Gr}_{\underline{e}}(\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(T,M)))x^{\operatorname{ind}_{T}M-\iota(\underline{e})}

for
M\in\mathcal{C}
(see Section
4.1
for more details).

In
[
6
]
, Caldero and Keller proved a higher-dimensional multiplication formula between cluster characters for the cluster category
\mathcal{C}_{Q}
of a Dynkin quiver
Q
. They showed that for any indecomposable objects
M,N\in\mathcal{C}_{Q}
,

\displaystyle\chi(\mathbb{P}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{Q}}(M,N[1]))X_{M}\cdot X_{N}

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{[L]}(\chi(\mathbb{P}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{Q}}(M,N[1])_{L[1]})+\chi(\mathbb{P}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{Q}}(N,M[1])_{L[1]}))X_{L},

where
\chi
is the Euler–Poincaré characteristic. The proof heavily depends on the 2-Calabi–Yau property of
\mathcal{C}_{Q}
. This multiplication formula was generalized firstly to acyclic quivers
[
22
,
24
]
and then to any 2-Calabi–Yau triangulated category with a cluster-tilting object
[
17
]
.

Inspired by the results in
[
6
]
, Geiss, Leclerc and Schröer
[
10
, Theorem 3]
proved an analogous formula for nilpotent module categories of preprojective algebras. Let
\Lambda_{Q}
be the preprojective algebra of an acyclic quiver
Q
and
\operatorname{nil}(\Lambda_{Q})
be the category of finite dimensional nilpotent
\Lambda_{Q}
-modules. Let
\mathfrak{n}
be the positive part of
\mathfrak{g}_{Q}
. Given two sequences
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{m})
,
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\dots,a_{m})
where
i_{j}\in\{1,\dots,n\}
and
a_{j}\in\{0,1\}
for
1\leqslant j\leqslant m
, Lusztig introduced the constructible functions
d_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}}\colon\operatorname{nil}(\Lambda_{Q})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
defined by

d_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}}(M)=\chi(\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}}(M)),

where
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}}(M)
is the set of flags of type
(\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a})
(see Section
2.4
for the definition). He proved that the enveloping algebra
U(\mathfrak{n})
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{M}=\bigoplus\mathcal{M}_{\underline{d}}
, where the
\mathcal{M}_{\underline{d}}
are certain vector spaces generated by
d_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}}
for proper pairs
(\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}).
Geiss, Leclerc and Schröer showed that the category
\operatorname{nil}(\Lambda_{Q})
has the Ext-symmetry, i.e.,

\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\Lambda_{Q}}(M,N)\cong D\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\Lambda_{Q}}(N,M)

for
M,N\in\operatorname{nil}(\Lambda_{Q})
. Let
\mathcal{M}^{*}
be the graded dual of
\mathcal{M}
. For
M\in\operatorname{nil}(\Lambda_{Q})
, they defined an evaluation form
\delta_{M}\in\mathcal{M}^{*}
, which is the map from
\mathcal{M}
to
\mathbb{Z}
mapping
d_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}}
to
d_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}}(M)
for any pair
(\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a})
. We denote by
\Lambda_{\underline{d}}
the variety of nilpotent
\Lambda
-modules of dimension vector
\underline{d}
and set
\langle M\rangle:=\{X\in\Lambda_{\underline{d}}\mid\delta_{X}=\delta_{M}\}
. Then there exists a finite subset
S(\underline{d})
of
\Lambda_{\underline{d}}
such that
\Lambda_{\underline{d}}=\bigsqcup_{M\in S(\underline{d})}\langle M\rangle
. For
M\in\Lambda_{\underline{d}}
and
N\in\Lambda_{\underline{d}^{\prime}}
, they obtained the following multiplication formula:

\chi\bigl(\mathbb{P}\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\Lambda_{Q}}(M,N)\bigr)\delta_{M}\cdot\delta_{N}=\sum_{L\in S(\underline{d})}\bigl(\chi\bigl(\mathbb{P}\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\Lambda_{Q}}(M,N)_{\langle L\rangle}\bigr)+\chi\bigl(\mathbb{P}\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\Lambda_{Q}}(N,M)_{\langle L\rangle}\bigr)\bigr)\delta_{L}.

The similarity between
\mathcal{C}_{Q}
and
\operatorname{nil}(\Lambda_{Q})
was further studied by many authors
[
2
,
11
]
. Let
W
be the Weyl group of
\mathfrak{g}_{Q}
. For
w\in W,
Buan, Iyama, Reiten, and Scott
[
2
]
constructed a 2-Calabi–Yau Frobenius subcategory
\mathcal{C}_{w}
of
\operatorname{nil}(\Lambda_{Q})
. When
w=(s_{n}\cdots s_{1})^{2}
, the stable category
\underline{\mathcal{C}_{w}}
of
\mathcal{C}_{w}
is just
\mathcal{C}_{Q}.
Given a reduced expression of
w=s_{i_{r}}\cdots s_{i_{1}}
and set
\mathbf{i}:=(i_{r},\dots,i_{1})
. For
M\in\mathcal{C}_{w}
,
\delta_{M}
can be reformulated into the following form:

\Delta_{M}=\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{N}^{r}}\delta_{M}(d_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}})t^{\mathbf{a}}=\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{N}^{r}}\chi(\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}}{(M)})t^{\mathbf{a}}.

Geiss, Leclerc and Schröer made explicit correspondences between the two multiplication formulas as follows. They defined a cluster tilting object
W_{\mathbf{i}}
in
\mathcal{C}_{w}
and set
A:=\underline{\mathrm{End}}_{\mathcal{C}_{w}}(W_{\mathbf{i}})^{\rm op}
. For
X\in\mathcal{C}_{w}
,
F(X)=\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\Lambda}(W_{\mathbf{i}},X)
is an
A
-module. There is a bijiection
d_{\mathbf{i},M}
between
\{\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{N}^{r}\mid\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}}(M)\neq\varnothing\}
and
\{\underline{d}\in\mathbb{N}^{n}\mid\operatorname{Gr}_{\underline{d}}(F(M))\neq\varnothing\}
. Furthermore, there is an isomorphism of algebraic varieties between
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}}(M)
and
\operatorname{Gr}_{d_{\mathbf{i},M}(\mathbf{a})}(F(M))
. These lead to a correspondence between
\Delta_{M}
and
X^{T}_{\underline{M}}
for
M\in\mathcal{C}_{w}
where
T=\underline{W_{\mathbf{i}}}
and
\underline{M}
are respectively the image of
W_{\mathbf{i}}
and
M
under the natural functor from
\mathcal{C}_{w}
to
\underline{\mathcal{C}_{w}}.

Quantum cluster algebras were introduced by Berenstein and Zelevinsky
[
1
]
as the quantization of cluster algebras. They are defined to be certain noncommutative algebras over
\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm}]
generated by quantum cluster variables for a compatible pair
(B,\Lambda)
of two matrices
B
and
\Lambda
. Rupel
[
21
]
defined a quantum cluster character as a quantum analogue of the Caldero–Chapoton map. Let
Q
be an acyclic valued quiver over a finite field
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{F}_{q}
. For
M\in\mathrm{Rep}_{\mathbb{F}}Q
,

X^{\mathbb{F}}_{M}=\sum_{\underline{e}}q^{-\frac{1}{2}\langle\underline{e},\underline{m}-\underline{e}\rangle}|\mathrm{Gr}_{\underline{e}}(M)|x^{-B\underline{e}-(I-R)\underline{m}}

(see Section
5
for the explicit definition). Qin
[
19
]
gave an alternative definition for the quantum cluster characters of rigid objects in the cluster category of an acyclic equally valued quiver. The definition does not involve the choice of the ground finite field. Then he proved that the definition is consistent with the mutation rules between quantum cluster variables, i.e., for indecomposable rigid objects
M
,
N
in
\mathcal{C}_{Q}
with
\operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{Q}}(M,N[1])=1
, we have

X^{\mathbb{F}}_{M}\cdot X^{\mathbb{F}}_{N}=q^{\frac{1}{2}\Lambda(\operatorname{ind}M,\operatorname{ind}L)}X^{\mathbb{F}}_{L}+q^{\frac{1}{2}\Lambda(\operatorname{ind}M,\operatorname{ind}L^{\prime})}X^{\mathbb{F}}_{L^{\prime}}.

The multiplication formula of quantum cluster characters was generalized in
[
8
]
. More generally, we give a quantum analogue of Caldero–Keller’s multiplication formula in Section
4
. Recently, another quantum analogue was proved in
[
7
]
. The exact relation between these two formulas will be discovered in the near future.

In contrast to the case of the cluster character, the general definition of the quantum cluster character is still unknown. The aim of this paper is to define the weighted quantum cluster functions for both an abelian category
\mathcal{A}
with the Ext-symmetry and a 2-Calabi–Yau triangulated category
\mathcal{C}
with a cluster tilting object. A weighted quantum cluster function is of the form
f*_{[\epsilon]}\delta_{L}
or
f*_{\epsilon}X_{L}
with a weight function
f
.
So far we do not know whether or not weighted quantum cluster functions can give quantum cluster characters which compute the quantum cluster variables
in the sense of Berenstein–Zelevinsky by taking the proper weights.

In this paper, we use the following notation: given a finite set
S
, and a function
g
on
S
, we define

\int_{x\in S}g(x):=\displaystyle\sum_{x\in S}g(x).

The first main result in this paper is the following theorem (cf. Theorem
3.25
for details).

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
{\rm Hom}
-finite,
\operatorname{Ext}
-finite abelian category with
\operatorname{Ext}
-symmetry over a finite field
k=\mathbb{F}_{q}
such that the iso-classes of objects form a set. For any weighted quantum cluster functions

f*_{[\epsilon^{\prime}]}\delta_{M}
and
g*_{[\epsilon^{\prime\prime}]}\delta_{N}
such that
\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}(M,N)\neq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\big|\mathbb{P}\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}(M,N)\big|(f*_{[\epsilon^{\prime}]}\delta_{M})*(g*_{[\epsilon^{\prime\prime}]}\delta_{N})

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{\mathbb{P}\epsilon\in\mathbb{P}\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}(M,N)}\bigl(f^{+}_{\rm ext}*_{[\epsilon]}\mathbb{S}_{fg}\bigr)*_{[\epsilon]}\delta_{{\rm mt}\epsilon}+\int_{\mathbb{P}\eta\in\mathbb{P}\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}(N,M)}(f_{\rm hom}*_{[\eta]}\mathbb{S}_{gf})*_{[\eta]}\delta_{{\rm mt}\eta}

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{\mathbb{P}\epsilon\in\mathbb{P}\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}(M,N)}\mathbb{S}_{fg}*_{[\epsilon]}\delta_{{\rm mt}\epsilon}+\int_{\mathbb{P}\eta\in\mathbb{P}\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}(N,M)}(f^{-}_{\rm ext}*_{[\eta]}f_{\rm hom}*_{[\eta]}\mathbb{S}_{gf})*_{[\eta]}\delta_{{\rm mt}\eta}.

This theorem gives the explicit multiplication between two weighted quantum cluster functions over an abelian category
\mathcal{A}
with the Ext-symmetry.

The second main result is as follows (cf. Theorem
4.23
for details).

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a
{\rm Hom}
-finite,
2
-Calabi–Yau, Krull–Schmidt triangulated category over a finite field
k=\mathbb{F}_{q}
with a cluster tilting object
T
. For any weighted quantum cluster functions

f*_{\epsilon^{\prime}}X_{M}
and
g*_{\epsilon^{\prime\prime}}X_{N}
such that
{\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(M,\Sigma N)\neq 0
, we have

\displaystyle|\mathbb{P}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(M,\Sigma N)|(f*_{\epsilon^{\prime}}X_{M})\cdot(g*_{\epsilon^{\prime\prime}}X_{N})

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{\mathbb{P}\epsilon\in\mathbb{P}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(M,\Sigma N)}\bigl(g^{+}_{\rm ext}*_{\epsilon}{f}_{\rm spec}*_{\epsilon}\mathbb{T}_{fg}\bigr)*_{\epsilon}X_{{\rm mt}\epsilon}

\displaystyle\phantom{\quad=}{}+\int_{\mathbb{P}\eta\in\mathbb{P}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(N,\Sigma M)}(g_{\rm skew}*_{\eta}{f}_{\rm spec}*_{\eta}\mathbb{T}_{gf})*_{\eta}X_{{\rm mt}\eta}

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{\mathbb{P}\epsilon\in\mathbb{P}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(M,\Sigma N)}({f}_{\rm spec}*_{\epsilon}\mathbb{T}_{fg})*_{\epsilon}X_{{\rm mt}\epsilon}

\displaystyle\phantom{\quad=}{}+\int_{\mathbb{P}\eta\in\mathbb{P}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(N,\Sigma M)}(g^{-}_{\rm ext}*_{\eta}g_{\rm skew}*_{\eta}{f}_{\rm spec}*_{\eta}\mathbb{T}_{gf})*_{\eta}X_{{\rm mt}\eta}.

This theorem gives the explicit multiplication between two weighted quantum cluster functions over a 2-Calabi–Yau triangulated category
\mathcal{C}
with a cluster tilting object
T
. It can be viewed as the quantum analogue of Caldero–Keller’s multiplication formula.

The third main result is the quantum analogue of the above Geiss–Leclerc–Schröer’s multiplication formula (cf. Theorem
5.14
for details).

Theorem 1.3
.

For any weighted quantum cluster functions
f*_{\epsilon^{\prime}}\Delta_{\mathbf{i},M}
and
g*_{\epsilon^{\prime\prime}}\Delta_{\mathbf{i},N}
such that
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{C}_{\omega}}(M,N)\neq 0
, in
A_{m,\lambda}
we have

\displaystyle\big|\mathbb{P}\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{C}_{\omega}}^{1}(M,N)\big|(f*_{\epsilon^{\prime}}\Delta_{\mathbf{i},M})\cdot(g*_{\epsilon^{\prime\prime}}\Delta_{\mathbf{i},N})

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{\mathbb{P}\epsilon\in\mathbb{P}\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{C}_{\omega}}^{1}(M,N)}\bigl(f^{+}_{\rm ext}*_{\epsilon}{f}_{\rm spec}*_{\epsilon}\mathbb{S}_{fg}\bigr)*_{\epsilon}\Delta_{\mathbf{i},{\rm mt}\epsilon}

\displaystyle\phantom{\quad=}{}+\int_{\mathbb{P}\eta\in\mathbb{P}\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{C}_{\omega}}^{1}(N,M)}(f_{\rm skew}*_{\eta}{f}_{\rm spec}*_{\eta}\mathbb{S}_{gf})*_{\eta}\Delta_{\mathbf{i},{\rm mt}\eta}

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{\mathbb{P}\epsilon\in\mathbb{P}\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{C}_{\omega}}^{1}(M,N)}({f}_{\rm spec}*_{\epsilon}\mathbb{S}_{fg})*_{\epsilon}\Delta_{\mathbf{i},{\rm mt}\epsilon}

\displaystyle\phantom{\quad=}{}+\int_{\mathbb{P}\eta\in\mathbb{P}\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{C}_{\omega}}^{1}(N,M)}(f^{-}_{\rm ext}*_{\eta}f_{\rm skew}*_{\eta}{f}_{\rm spec}*_{\eta}\mathbb{S}_{gf})*_{\eta}\Delta_{\mathbf{i},{\rm mt}\eta}.

The fourth main result focuses on weighted quantum cluster functions from hereditary algebras. By choosing a particular weight function, we recover Qin’s result
[
18
, Proposition 5.4.1]
(cf. Theorem
6.22
for details).

Theorem 1.4
.

In the cluster category
\mathcal{C}=D^{b}\bigl(\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\bigr)/{\tau^{-1}\Sigma}
of a hereditary algebra
\widetilde{A}
, given two indecomposable coefficient-free rigid
objects
M
,
N\in\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}
with

\operatorname{dim}_{k}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(M,\Sigma N)=1

and two non-split triangles

N\rightarrow L\rightarrow M\xrightarrow{\epsilon}\Sigma N\qquad\text{and}\qquad M\rightarrow L^{\prime}\rightarrow N\xrightarrow{\eta}\Sigma M,

where
L^{\prime}
is located in the fundamental domain, then we have

\tilde{X}_{M}\cdot\tilde{X}_{N}=q^{\frac{1}{2}\lambda(\operatorname{ind}M,\operatorname{ind}N)-\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\tilde{X}_{L}+q^{\frac{1}{2}\lambda(\operatorname{ind}M,\operatorname{ind}N)}\cdot\tilde{X}_{L^{\prime}}.

Recently, Keller, Plamondon and Qin gave a refined multiplication formula for cluster characters over
2
-Calabi–Yau triangulated category with tilting objects
[
15
]
. Its quantum analogue has been obtained in
[
23
]

via a motivic version of weighted quantum cluster functions.

The paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we first define a chain of monomorphisms as a generalization of flag and then define a set of chains of monomorphisms of certain type. In Section
3
, we introduce the quantum cluster function attached to an abelian category with the Ext-symmetry in a general context. Corollaries
3.3
and
3.7
play an important role in characterizing the quantum cluster function. Then we define the weight function and the weighted quantum cluster function. By choosing appropriate pair of weight functions, we prove the multiplication formula of weighted quantum cluster functions in Section
3.9
. Section
4
is devoted to defining the weighted quantum cluster function for a 2-Calabi–Yau triangulated category with a cluster tilting object. Lemma
4.8
helps to describe the structure of the quantum cluster function. We prove the multiplication formula of weighted quantum cluster functions in Section
4.9
. Section
5
establishes the explicit connection between the two multiplication formulas as stated in Theorem
1.1
and Theorem
1.2
. We make a correspondence between weighted quantum cluster functions in Section
3
and Section
4
by applying Geiss–Leclerc–Schröer’s correspondence as above. In Section
6
, we show that Theorem
4.23
recovers the multiplcation formula in
[
18
]
by assigning proper weight functions.

2
Chains of monomorphisms

2.1
Chains of morphisms

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
{\operatorname{Hom}}
-finite,
\operatorname{Ext}
-finite abelian category with
\operatorname{Ext}
-symmetry over a finite field
k=\mathbb{F}_{q}
such that the isoclasses of objects form a set.
We assume that
\mathcal{A}
has finitely many simple objects up to isomorphism and fix a complete set of simple objects
\{S_{1},\dots,S_{n}\}
up to isomorphism in
\mathcal{A}
.
For a fixed
m\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote by
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{\rm mor}_{m}
the set of all chains of morphisms

\mathbf{c}=\bigl(L_{m}\xrightarrow{\iota_{\mathbf{c},m}}L_{m-1}\longrightarrow\dots\longrightarrow L_{1}\xrightarrow{\iota_{\mathbf{c},1}}L_{0}\bigr),

where
L_{0},\dots,L_{m}\in\mathcal{A}
and
\iota_{\mathbf{c},1},\dots,\iota_{\mathbf{c},m}\in\rm{Mor}\mathcal{A}
.

Using the isomorphism relations
L\cong L^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{A}
, we can induce the isomorphism relations in
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{\rm mor}_{m}
.

Definition 2.1
.

Two chains of morphisms
\mathbf{c}=\bigl(L_{m}\xrightarrow{\iota_{\mathbf{c},m}}L_{m-1}\longrightarrow\dots\longrightarrow L_{1}\xrightarrow{\iota_{\mathbf{c},1}}L_{0}\bigr)
and
\mathbf{c}^{\prime}=\bigl(L^{\prime}_{m}\xrightarrow{\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},m}}L^{\prime}_{m-1}\longrightarrow\dots\longrightarrow L^{\prime}_{1}\xrightarrow{\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},1}}L^{\prime}_{0}\bigr)
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{\rm mor}_{m}
are called isomorphic if there are isomorphisms
L_{i}\xrightarrow{f_{i}}L^{\prime}_{j}
in
\rm{Mor}\mathcal{A}
such that
f_{i-1}\circ\iota_{\mathbf{c},i}=\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},i}\circ f_{i}
, i.e., we have the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{m-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\prime}_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\prime}_{m-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\prime}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\prime}_{0}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathbf{c},m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathbf{c},m-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{m-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathbf{c},1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},m-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},1}}

We denote the set of isomorphism classes in
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{\rm mor}_{m}
by
\mathcal{F}^{\rm mor}_{m}
and we still write
\mathbf{c}
for its isomorphism class in
\mathcal{F}^{\rm mor}_{m}
.

2.2
Exact structure

Given a short exact sequence

\textstyle{\epsilon\colon\ 0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

in
\mathcal{A}
, write
[\epsilon]
for its equivalence class in
\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}(M,N)
. Recall that two short exact sequences

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{1}}
\textstyle{L\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{1}}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

and

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{2}}
\textstyle{L^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{2}}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

correspond to the same element in
\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}(M,N)
if and only if there is an
\mathcal{A}
-isomorphism
{a\colon L\longrightarrow L^{\prime}}
with
f_{2}=af_{1}
and
g_{2}=g_{1}a^{-1}
, i.e., we have the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{2}}
\textstyle{L^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{2}}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\,\,}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{1}}
\textstyle{L\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{a}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

Then any morphism
\lambda\in{\rm Mor}(N,N^{\prime})
induces an equivalence class
\lambda\circ[\epsilon]
in
\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}(M,N^{\prime})
which is called the pushout of
[\epsilon]
along
\lambda
. Similarly, for any
\rho\in{\rm Mor}(M^{\prime\prime},M)
, there is an equivalence class
[\epsilon]\circ\rho
in
\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}(M^{\prime\prime},N)
which is called the pullback of
[\epsilon]
along
\rho
. We have the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{\lambda\circ[\epsilon]\colon\quad 0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\,\,}
\textstyle{[\epsilon]\colon\quad}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda}
\textstyle{L\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\,\,}
\textstyle{[\epsilon]\circ\rho\colon\quad 0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L^{\prime\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M^{\prime\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{0.}

Conversely, given

\displaystyle[\epsilon^{\prime}]\in\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}(M^{\prime},N^{\prime}),\qquad[\epsilon^{\prime\prime}]\in\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}(M^{\prime\prime},N^{\prime\prime}),\qquad\lambda\in{\rm Mor}(N^{\prime\prime},N^{\prime}),

\displaystyle\rho\in{\rm Mor}(M^{\prime\prime},M^{\prime})

as in the diagram

\textstyle{[\epsilon^{\prime}]\colon\quad 0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\,\,}
\textstyle{[\epsilon^{\prime\prime}]\colon\quad 0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N^{\prime\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda}
\textstyle{L^{\prime\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M^{\prime\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{0,}

we can complete the commutative diagram by adding an appropriate morphism in
{\rm Mor}(L^{\prime\prime},L^{\prime})
if and only if the pushout and pullback coincide, i.e.,

\lambda\circ[\epsilon^{\prime\prime}]=[\epsilon^{\prime}]\circ\rho.

Moreover, in this case, the morphism in
{\rm Mor}(L^{\prime\prime},L^{\prime})
is given by
g\circ f
as shown in the following diagram:

\textstyle{[\epsilon^{\prime}]\colon}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\,\,}
\textstyle{\lambda\circ[\epsilon^{\prime\prime}]=[\epsilon^{\prime}]\circ\rho\colon}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{M^{\prime\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{0\,\,}
\textstyle{[\epsilon^{\prime\prime}]\colon}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N^{\prime\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda}
\textstyle{L^{\prime\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{M^{\prime\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

The equality
[\epsilon^{\prime}]\circ\rho=\lambda\circ[\epsilon^{\prime}]
can be illustrated by the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{[\epsilon^{\prime}]\colon}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\,\,}
\textstyle{[\epsilon^{\prime}]\circ\rho\colon}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widetilde{L}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{M^{\prime\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{0\,\,}
\textstyle{\lambda\circ[\epsilon^{\prime}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\colon}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N^{\prime\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{M^{\prime\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\,\,}
\textstyle{[\epsilon^{\prime\prime}]\colon}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N^{\prime\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda}
\textstyle{L^{\prime\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M^{\prime\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

Since every
[\epsilon]\in\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}(M,N)
can be represented by

\textstyle{\epsilon\colon\ 0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{0}

with

,

,

being unique up to isomorphism, we denote

,
{\rm st}\epsilon:=N
and
{\rm mt}\epsilon:=L
in the following sections.

Now, more generally, let

for

, and take two isomorphism classes of chains of morphisms

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}^{\prime}=\bigl(M_{m}\xrightarrow{\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},m}}M_{m-1}\longrightarrow\dots\longrightarrow M_{1}\xrightarrow{\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},1}}M_{0}=M\bigr),

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}=\bigl(N_{m}\xrightarrow{\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},m}}N_{m-1}\longrightarrow\dots\longrightarrow N_{1}\xrightarrow{\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},1}}N_{0}=N\bigr)

0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 65.51208pt\raise-105.75pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.11528pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},m}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 91.42424pt\raise-96.75pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 133.46538pt\raise-121.75pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{L_{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 184.54765pt\raise-121.75pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 184.54765pt\raise-121.75pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{M_{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 236.55344pt\raise-121.75pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 196.74153pt\raise-105.75pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.11528pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},m}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 196.74153pt\raise-96.75pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 236.55344pt\raise-121.75pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0.}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{split}

(2.1)

In order to decide whether there exists a chain of morphisms

\mathbf{c}=\bigl(L_{m}\xrightarrow{\iota_{\mathbf{c},m}}L_{m-1}\longrightarrow\dots\longrightarrow L_{1}\xrightarrow{\iota_{\mathbf{c},1}}L_{0}=L\bigr)

to complete the above commutative diagram, we introduce a linear map

\displaystyle\beta_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}}\colon\ \bigoplus_{j=0}^{m}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(M_{j},N_{j})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{j=0}^{m-1}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(M_{j+1},N_{j}),

\displaystyle([\epsilon_{0}],\dots,[\epsilon_{m}])

\displaystyle\longmapsto([\epsilon_{j-1}]\circ\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},j}-\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},j}\circ[\epsilon_{j}],1\leqslant j\leqslant m).

The definition and some properties of maps
\beta_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}}
and
\beta^{\prime}_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime}}
, which will be introduced later as the
linear dual of
\beta_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}}
, were firstly given in
[
6
]
for module categories.
We generalize these two maps to abelian categories and prove some necessary properties here.

Lemma 2.2
.

There exists a chain of morphisms

\mathbf{c}=\bigl(L_{m}\xrightarrow{\iota_{\mathbf{c},m}}L_{m-1}\longrightarrow\dots\longrightarrow L_{1}\xrightarrow{\iota_{\mathbf{c},1}}L_{0}=L\bigr),

which can complete the commutative diagram (
2.1
) if and only if

([\epsilon_{0}],\dots,[\epsilon_{m}])\in\operatorname{Ker}\beta_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}}.

Proof.

A family of elements
([\epsilon_{0}],\dots,[\epsilon_{m}])\in\operatorname{Ker}\beta_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}}
is exactly one which makes pushout and pullback coincide at every level.
∎

In this case, we get the morphism
\iota_{L,i}
by composing two vertical morphisms in the following diagram:

\textstyle{[\epsilon_{i-1}]\colon}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N_{i-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L_{i-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M_{i-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\,\,}
\textstyle{\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},i}\circ[\epsilon_{i}]=[\epsilon_{i-1}]\circ\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},i}\colon}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N_{i-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L^{\prime}_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{M_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},i}}
\textstyle{0\,\,}
\textstyle{[\epsilon_{i}]\colon}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},i}}
\textstyle{L_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{M_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

We denote this assignment by

\displaystyle B_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}}\colon\ \operatorname{Ker}\beta_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}^{\rm mor}_{m},

\displaystyle([\epsilon_{0}],\dots,[\epsilon_{m}])

\displaystyle\longmapsto(L_{m}\xrightarrow{\iota_{\mathbf{c},m}}L_{m-1}\longrightarrow\dots\longrightarrow L_{1}\xrightarrow{\iota_{\mathbf{c},1}}L_{0}=L).

Lemma 2.3
.

For any chains of morphisms
\mathbf{c}^{\prime}
,
\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}
, the map
B_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}}
is well defined and injective.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the discussion in the case when
m=1
. The choice of short exact sequences representing
([\epsilon_{0}],\dots,[\epsilon_{m}])
is not unique, but is unique up to the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{\epsilon_{i}\colon\quad 0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\,\,}
\textstyle{\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}\colon\quad 0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L^{\prime}_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{i}}
\textstyle{M_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

where
a_{i}
is an isomorphism. Then we get two chains of morphisms
B_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}}([\epsilon_{0}],\dots,[\epsilon_{m}])
and
B_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}}([\epsilon^{\prime}_{0}],\dots,[\epsilon^{\prime}_{m}])
. One can check they are isomorphic in
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{\rm mor}_{m}
through the family of isomorphisms
a_{i}
.

Conversely, given two choices of exact sequences which induce the same chain of morphisms in
\mathcal{F}^{\rm mor}_{m}
, then the isomorphism
L_{i}\rightarrow L^{\prime}_{i}
also give the equivalence between the two exact sequences:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\prime}_{i-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i-1}\,\,}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\prime}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{i-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i-1}.\qed}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

Since all extension groups considered here are finite dimensional over a finite field, we have

Corollary 2.4
.

Given chains of morphisms
\mathbf{c}^{\prime}
,
\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}
, the map
B_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}}\colon\operatorname{Ker}\beta_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Im}B_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}}
is a bijection and
|\operatorname{Im}B_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}}|=|\operatorname{Ker}\beta_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}}|
.

2.3
Chains of monomorphisms

Definition 2.5
.

Given a chain of morphisms

\mathbf{c}=\bigl(L_{m}\xrightarrow{\iota_{\mathbf{c},m}}L_{m-1}\longrightarrow\dots\longrightarrow L_{1}\xrightarrow{\iota_{\mathbf{c},1}}L_{0}\bigr)

in
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{\rm mor}_{m}
, it is called a chain of monomorphisms if all
\iota_{\mathbf{c},i}
are monomorphisms and
L_{m}=0
.

One can easily check this definition is independent of choice of chain in an isomorphism class and we denote by
\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{m}
the subset of
\mathcal{F}^{\rm mor}_{m}
consisting of all isomorphism classes of chains of monomorphisms. We also denote by
\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{m,L}
the subset of
\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{m}
consisting of all isomorphism classes of chains of monomorphisms with
L_{0}\cong L
.

Lemma 2.6
.

If
\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{m}
, then
\operatorname{Im}B_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}}\subseteq\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{m}
.

Proof.

By the snake lemma, all rows in the following commutative diagram are exact:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{j-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{j-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{j-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\,\,}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Ker}\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Ker}\iota_{\mathbf{c},j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Ker}\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},j}}}

Since
\mathbf{c}^{\prime}
,
\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}
are chains of monomorphisms,
\operatorname{Ker}\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},j}=0
and
\operatorname{Ker}\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},j}=0
. So is
\operatorname{Ker}\iota_{\mathbf{c},j}
.
∎

2.4
Type

Recall that
\mathcal{A}
admits a complete set simple objects
\{S_{1},\dots,S_{n}\}
up to isomorphism, so we can consider composition factors of an object in
\mathcal{A}
with finite length.

Definition 2.7
.

Given a chain of monomorphisms

\mathbf{c}=\bigl(L_{m}\xrightarrow{\iota_{\mathbf{c},m}}L_{m-1}\longrightarrow\dots\longrightarrow L_{1}\xrightarrow{\iota_{\mathbf{c},1}}L_{0}\bigr)

and two sequences
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{m})
,
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\dots,a_{m})
, where
i_{j}\in\{1,\dots,n\}
,
a_{j}\in\{0,1\}
,
\mathbf{c}
is called of type
(\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a})
if
\operatorname{Coker}\iota_{\mathbf{c},j}\cong S_{i_{j}}
when
a_{j}=1
and
0
otherwise for
1\leqslant j\leqslant m
.

We denote the set of all chains of monomorphisms of type
(\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a})
by
\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}}
and the set of all chains of monomorphisms of type
(\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a})
with
L_{0}\cong L
by
\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a},L}
.

Lemma 2.8
.

If
(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime})\in\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}^{\prime},M}\times\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime},N}
and
a^{\prime}_{j}+a^{\prime\prime}_{j}\leqslant 1
for
1\leqslant j\leqslant m
, then

\operatorname{Im}B_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}}\subseteq\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}^{\prime}+\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime}}.

Proof.

Since
a^{\prime}_{j}+a^{\prime\prime}_{j}\leqslant 1
for
1\leqslant j\leqslant m
,
\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}^{\prime}+\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime}}
is well defined.
By the snake lemma, we have an exact sequence

\operatorname{Ker}\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},j}\rightarrow\operatorname{Coker}\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},j}\rightarrow\operatorname{Coker}\iota_{\mathbf{c},j}\rightarrow\operatorname{Coker}\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},j}\rightarrow 0.

Since
\mathbf{c}^{\prime}
is a chain of monomorphisms,
\operatorname{Ker}\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},j}=0
. So the composition factors of
\operatorname{Coker}\iota_{\mathbf{c},j}
are the union of composition factors of
\operatorname{Coker}\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},j}
and
\operatorname{Coker}\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},j}
, which are one copy of
S_{i_{j}}
.
∎

If
\mathbf{a}=(1,\dots,1)
, we simply write
\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i}}
instead of
\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}}
.

3
Abelian categories with
\boldsymbol{\operatorname{Ext}}
-symmetry

and the multiplication formula

3.1
Quantum cluster function

Now we introduce the concept of the quantum cluster functions over
\mathcal{A}
.

Let
d_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}}
be a formal notation representing a type of chains of monomorphisms and

\mathcal{M}_{q}:=\bigoplus_{(\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a})}\mathbb{Q}d_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}}

be the
\mathbb{Q}
-space spanned by all
d_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}}
.

For each object
L
in
\mathcal{A}
, we define a
\mathbb{Q}
-valued linear function
\delta_{L}
on
\mathcal{M}_{q}
by
\delta_{L}(d_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}})=|\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a},L}|
and call such a function the quantum cluster function of
L
.

The core purpose of this section is to study the relationship between quantum cluster functions of objects in
\mathcal{A}
related by a short exact sequence.

3.2
Mappings with affine fibers

We denote by

\operatorname{EF}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}}(M,N):=\big\{([\epsilon],\mathbf{c})\mid[\epsilon]\in\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}(M,N),\,\mathbf{c}\in\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a},{\rm mt}\epsilon}\big\}

the set of all pairs of extensions of
M
,
N
and chain of monomorphisms of the middle term of type
(\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a})
.

From
([\epsilon],\mathbf{c})\in\operatorname{EF}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}}(M,N)
, we can induce two chains of monomorphisms ending with
M
,
N
respectively. More precisely, we have

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\,\,}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\,\,}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{m-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{m-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{m-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\,\,}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathbf{c},1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{m-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{m-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathbf{c},m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},m}}

where

N_{j}=i^{-1}(\operatorname{Im}\iota_{\mathbf{c},1}\circ\dots\circ\iota_{\mathbf{c},j}),\qquad M_{j}=p(\operatorname{Im}\iota_{\mathbf{c},1}\circ\dots\circ\iota_{\mathbf{c},j}),

\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},j}
,
\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},j}
are natural embeddings and
i_{j}
,
p_{j}
are naturally induced by
i
and
p
respectively for
{1\leqslant j\leqslant m}
.

We write this assignment as

\phi_{MN}\colon\ \operatorname{EF}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}}(M,N)\rightarrow\coprod_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}+\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime}=\mathbf{a}}\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}^{\prime},M}\times\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime},N}.

The coproduct runs over all pairs
(\mathbf{a}^{\prime},\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime})
with
\mathbf{a}^{\prime}+\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime}=\mathbf{a}
since the composition factor at each level is fixed by
\mathbf{a}
.

Given two chains of monomorphisms
\mathbf{c}^{\prime}
,
\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}
ending with
M
and
N
respectively, we are interested in the fiber
\phi^{-1}_{MN}(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime})
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Consider chains of monomorphisms
\mathbf{c}^{\prime}
,
\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}
ending with
M
,
N
respectively.
There exists a bijection between
\operatorname{Ker}\beta_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}}
and
\phi^{-1}_{MN}(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime})
, given by

([\epsilon_{0}],\dots,[\epsilon_{m}])\longmapsto([\epsilon_{0}],B_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}}([\epsilon_{0}],\dots,[\epsilon_{m}])).

Proof.

Consider chains of monomorphisms
\mathbf{c}^{\prime}
,
\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}
ending with
M
,
N
respectively and
[\epsilon_{0}]\in\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}(M,N)
with
{\rm mt}\epsilon_{0}=L
as in the
diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{0}\colon\quad 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{m-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{m-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},m}}

For any
\mathbf{c}\in\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{m,L}
, the following two statements are equivalent:

(1)

there are extensions
([\epsilon_{1}],\dots,[\epsilon_{m}])
satisfying
([\epsilon_{0}],[\epsilon_{1}],\dots,[\epsilon_{m}])\in\operatorname{Ker}\beta_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}}
such that
B_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}}([\epsilon_{0}],[\epsilon_{1}],\dots,[\epsilon_{m}])=\mathbf{c}
. By definition of
B_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}}
, this means that there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{0}\colon}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\,\,}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{1}\colon}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\,\,}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{m-1}\colon}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{m-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{m-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{m-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\,\,}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{m}\colon}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathbf{c},1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{m-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{m-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathbf{c},m}}

where
\iota_{\mathbf{c},i}
are given by composing the middle vertical morphisms in diagrams of the form

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N_{i-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L_{i-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M_{i-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\,\,}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N_{i-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L^{\prime}_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{M_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},i}}
\textstyle{0\,\,}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},i}}
\textstyle{L_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{M_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0;}

(2)

\phi_{MN}([\epsilon_{0}],\mathbf{c})=(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime})
. By definition of
\phi_{MN}
, this means that there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{0}\colon}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0,}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{m-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{m-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{m-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathbf{c},1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{m-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{m-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathbf{c},m}}

where
N_{j}=i_{j}^{-1}(L_{j})
,
M_{j}=p_{j}(L_{j})
, and
i_{j}
and
p_{j}
are restrictions of
i
and
p
.

Assume (1) is true. Since all rows in
(1)
are short exact sequences, we have
N_{j}=i_{j}^{-1}(L_{j})
and
M_{j}=p_{j}(L_{j})
, i.e.,
(2)
holds.

Conversely, given
(2)
, by definitions of
N_{j}
and
M_{j}
, we know that
N_{j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i_{j}}}{{\longrightarrow}}L_{j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{j}}}{{\longrightarrow}}M_{j}
is a short exact sequence for
1\leqslant j\leqslant m
.
Then we obtain a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{0}\colon}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\,\,}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{1}\colon}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\,\,}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{m-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{m-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{m-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{m-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\,\,}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{m}\colon}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathbf{c},1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{m-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{m-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathbf{c},m}}

which consists of chains of monomorphisms as columns and short exact sequences as rows. From the uniqueness of pushouts and pullbacks, the middle column
\mathbf{c}
must be isomorphic to
B_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}}([\epsilon_{0}],[\epsilon_{1}],\dots,[\epsilon_{m}])
where
([\epsilon_{1}],\dots,[\epsilon_{m}])
is as shown in the diagram.
∎

Let
[\epsilon]\in\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}(M,N)
with
{\rm mt}\epsilon=L
, and let
\mathbf{c}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{m,M}
,
\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{m,N}
. Then the chains
\mathbf{c}\in\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{m,L}

such that
\phi_{MN}([\epsilon],\mathbf{c})=(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime})
are precisely those of the form
B_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}}([\epsilon],\dots,[\epsilon_{m}])
with
([\epsilon],\dots,[\epsilon_{m}])\in\operatorname{Ker}\beta_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}}
.

On the other hand, note that
\phi_{MN}
describes the relationship among
\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{m,L}
,
\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{m,M}
and
\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{m,N}
, and hence the relationship among characters
\delta_{M}
,
\delta_{N}
and
\delta_{L}
. In order to describe the structure more clearly, we need to refine this map.

Fix
[\epsilon]\in\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{A}}(M,N)
, and define

\phi_{[\epsilon]}:=\phi_{MN}([\epsilon],-)\colon\ \mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a},L}\rightarrow\coprod_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}+\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime}=\mathbf{a}}\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}^{\prime},M}\times\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime},N},

where
L={\rm mt}\epsilon
and write the preimage as

\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a},L}([\epsilon],\mathbf{a}^{\prime},\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime})=\phi_{[\epsilon]}^{-1}(\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}^{\prime},M}\times\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime},N}).

Since the coproduct

\coprod_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}+\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime}=\mathbf{a}}\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}^{\prime},M}\times\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime},N}

is a disjoint union, we naturally have

\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a},L}=\coprod_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}+\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime}=\mathbf{a}}\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a},L}([\epsilon],\mathbf{a}^{\prime},\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime}).

However, the structure of a fiber of

\phi_{[\epsilon]}\colon\ \mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a},L}([\epsilon],\mathbf{a}^{\prime},\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime})\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}^{\prime},M}\times\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime},N}

is heavily dependent on the relation between
[\epsilon]
and specific chains
(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime})
.

The following corollary describes the image of
\phi_{[\epsilon]}
restricted to
\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a},L}([\epsilon],\mathbf{a}^{\prime},\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime})
.

Corollary 3.2
.

Given
(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime})
in
\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}^{\prime},M}\times\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime},N}
,

(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime})\in\phi_{[\epsilon]}(\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a},L}([\epsilon],\mathbf{a}^{\prime},\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime}))\qquad\text{if and only if}\quad[\epsilon]\in p_{0}\operatorname{Ker}\beta_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}},

where
p_{0}
is the projection from
\bigoplus_{j=0}^{m}\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}(M_{j},N_{j})
to
\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}(M,N)
.

Now, in the case that
[\epsilon]\in p_{0}\operatorname{Ker}\beta_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}}
, we calculate the fiber
\phi^{-1}_{[\epsilon]}(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime})
.

From Lemma
2.3
, we know that the assignment
B_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}}
which maps a family of extensions
([\epsilon_{0}],\dots,[\epsilon_{m}])
to a chain of monomorphisms ending with
L={\rm mt}\epsilon_{0}
is injective. We denote

K(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},[\epsilon]):=\{([\epsilon_{0}],\dots,[\epsilon_{m}])\in\operatorname{Ker}\beta_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}}|[\epsilon_{0}]=[\epsilon]\}.

Then we have

\operatorname{Ker}\beta_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}}=\coprod_{[\epsilon]\in p_{0}\operatorname{Ker}\beta_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}}}K(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},[\epsilon]).

Then by Lemma
3.1
, we have

\phi^{-1}_{[\epsilon]}(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime})\neq\varnothing
only if
[\epsilon]\in p_{0}\operatorname{Ker}\beta_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}}
and obtain the immediate corollary

Corollary 3.3
.

Given
(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime})
in
\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}^{\prime},M}\times\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime},N}
,

(1)

B_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}}\colon K(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},[\epsilon])\rightarrow\phi^{-1}_{[\epsilon]}(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime})
is bijective;

(2)

If
[\epsilon]\notin p_{0}\operatorname{Ker}\beta_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}}
,
\phi^{-1}_{[\epsilon]}(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime})=\varnothing
.

3.3
Dual case

Recall that the abelian category
\mathcal{A}
considered here admits
\operatorname{Ext}
-symmetry. That is to say, for any two objects
M
,
N
in
\mathcal{A}
, there is a natural isomorphism

E_{M,N}\colon\ \operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}(M,N)\cong D\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}(N,M),

where
D={\rm Hom}_{k}(-,k)
is the linear dual. That is

Proposition 3.4
.

Given
\lambda\in\operatorname{Hom}(N,N^{\prime})
,
\rho\in\operatorname{Hom}(M^{\prime},M)
,
[\epsilon]\in\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}(M,N)
and
[\eta]\in\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}(N^{\prime},M^{\prime})
, we have

E_{M^{\prime},N^{\prime}}(\lambda\circ[\epsilon]\circ\rho)([\eta])=E_{M,N}([\epsilon])(\rho\circ[\eta]\circ\lambda).

Consider the linear map

\displaystyle\beta_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}}\colon\ \bigoplus_{j=0}^{m}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(M_{j},N_{j})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{j=0}^{m-1}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(M_{j+1},N_{j}),

\displaystyle([\epsilon_{0}],\dots,[\epsilon_{m}])

\displaystyle\longmapsto([\epsilon_{j-1}]\circ\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},j}-\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},j}\circ[\epsilon_{j}],1\leqslant j\leqslant m).

Since it is a linear map between finite dimensional spaces, we can calculate the dual of
\beta_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}}
explicitly using Proposition
3.4

\displaystyle\beta^{\prime}_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime}}=D\beta_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}}\colon\ \bigoplus_{j=0}^{m-1}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(N_{j},M_{j+1})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{j=0}^{m}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(N_{j},M_{j}),

\displaystyle([\eta_{0}],\dots,[\eta_{m-1}])

\displaystyle\longmapsto(\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},j+1}\circ[\eta_{j}]-[\eta_{j-1}]\circ\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},j},0\leqslant j\leqslant m),

where
[\eta_{-1}]:=\mathbf{0}
and
[\eta_{m}]:=\mathbf{0}
.

To decide whether a pair of chains
(\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime})
is located in the image of
\phi_{[\eta]}
, we need

Lemma 3.5
.

Given
(\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime},N}\times\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}^{\prime},M}
,

(\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime})\in\phi_{[\eta]}(\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a},L}([\eta],\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{a}^{\prime}))\qquad\text{if and only if}\quad[\eta]\in\operatorname{Im}\beta^{\prime}_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime}}\cap\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}(N,M),

where the intersection is realized through regarding
\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}(N,M)
as a linear subspace of
\bigoplus_{j=0}^{m}

\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}(N_{j},M_{j})
.

Proof.

By definition of
\beta^{\prime}_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime}}
,
[\eta]\in\operatorname{Im}\beta^{\prime}_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime}}\cap\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}(N,M)
if and only if there exists a family of extensions
([\eta_{0}],\dots,[\eta_{m-1}])
such that

(1)

\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},1}\circ[\eta_{0}]=[\eta]
;

(2)

\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},j+1}\circ[\eta_{j}]=[\eta_{j-1}]\circ\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},j}
for
1\leqslant j\leqslant m-1
;

(3)

[\eta_{m-1}]\circ\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},m}=0
.

The third condition is always satisfied since
\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}(N_{m},M_{m})=\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}(0,0)=0
.

Since
\mathbf{a}^{\prime}+\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime}=\mathbf{a}
is a
0
-
1
sequence, either
\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},j}
or
\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},j}
is an isomorphism. In either case, one can check that the first and second condition are equivalent to the condition that the pushout of
[\eta_{j}]
along
\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},j}
coincides with the pullback of
[\eta_{j-1}]
along
\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},j}
and
the pushout of extension between
M_{j}
and
N_{j}
along
\iota_{M,j}
coincides with the pullback of extension between
M_{j-1}
and
N_{j-1}
along
\iota_{N,j}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{j-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{j-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{j-1}\,\,}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{j},}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{j}.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{j+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rm pullback}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rm pushout}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[\eta_{j-1}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},j+1}\circ[\eta_{j}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[\eta_{j-1}]\circ\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[\eta_{j}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},j+1}}

According to Lemma
3.1
, the condition that the pullback and the pushout coincide ensures the preimage
\phi_{[\eta]}^{-1}(\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime})
is non-empty.
∎

Remark 3.6
.

In fact, we can check that

(\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime})\in\phi_{[\eta]}(\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a},L}([\eta],\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{a}^{\prime}))\qquad\text{ if and only if }\quad[\eta]\in p_{0}\operatorname{Ker}\beta_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime}}

as in Corollary
3.2
.

By Lemmas
3.1
and
3.5
, we obtain the dual of Corollary
3.3
.

Corollary 3.7
.

Given
(\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime},N}\times\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}^{\prime},M}
,

(1)

B_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime}}\colon K(\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime},[\eta])\rightarrow\phi^{-1}_{[\eta]}(\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime})
is bijective;

(2)

If
[\eta]\notin\operatorname{Im}\beta^{\prime}_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime}}\cap\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}(N,M)
,
\phi^{-1}_{[\eta]}(\mathbf{c}_{N},\mathbf{c}_{M})=\varnothing
.

3.4
Cardinality

Based on several lemmas above, we can refine the calculation of
\delta_{L}
. Recall that all extension groups are finite dimensional over a finite field. Since

\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a},L}=\coprod_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}+\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime}=\mathbf{a}}\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a},L}([\epsilon],\mathbf{a}^{\prime},\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime}),

we can write

|\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a},L}|=\int_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}+\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime}=\mathbf{a}}|\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a},L}([\epsilon],\mathbf{a}^{\prime},\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime})|.

Note that the decomposition of
\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a},L}
depends on the choice of the short exact sequence
\epsilon
.

By linear algebra,
|K(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},[\epsilon])|=|K(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},{\bf 0})|
if
[\epsilon]\in p_{0}\operatorname{Ker}\beta_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}}
. Note that
K(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},{\bf 0})
is a vector space over
k
so we can
define
k(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}):=\operatorname{dim}_{k}K(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},{\bf 0})
.

Moreover, Corollary
3.3
shows that if
[\epsilon]\notin p_{0}\operatorname{Ker}\beta_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}}
, then
|\phi^{-1}_{[\epsilon]}(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime})|=0
, and otherwise

|\phi^{-1}_{[\epsilon]}(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime})|=|K(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},[\epsilon])|=q^{k(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime})}
. So if
{\rm mt}\epsilon=L
, we have

\displaystyle\delta_{L}(d_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}})

\displaystyle=|\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a},L}|=\int_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}+\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime}=\mathbf{a}}|\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a},L}([\epsilon],\mathbf{a}^{\prime},\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime})|

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}+\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime}=\mathbf{a},(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime})\in\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}^{\prime},M}\times\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime},N}}\big|\phi^{-1}_{[\epsilon]}(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime})\big|

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}+\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime}=\mathbf{a},(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime})\in\phi_{[\epsilon]}(\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a},L}([\epsilon],\mathbf{a}^{\prime},\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime}))}q^{k(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime})},

where all integrals are finite sums.

Remark 3.8
.

Since different
[\epsilon]
,
[\epsilon^{\prime}]
in
\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}(M,N)
may admit the same middle term
{\rm mt}\epsilon={\rm mt}\epsilon^{\prime}=L
, the refinement of
\delta_{L}(d_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}})
in the above equation depends on
the choice of
[\epsilon]
.

Moreover, Corollary
3.7
shows if
[\eta]\notin\operatorname{Im}\beta^{\prime}_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime}}\cap\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}(N,M)
,
|\phi^{-1}_{[\eta]}(\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime})|=0
and if
[\eta]\in\operatorname{Im}\beta^{\prime}_{\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime}}\cap\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}(N,M)
,

|\phi^{-1}_{[\eta]}(\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime})|=|K(\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime},[\eta])|=q^{k(\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime})}
. So if
{\rm mt}\eta=L
, we have

\displaystyle\delta_{L}(d_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}})

\displaystyle=|\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a},L}|=\int_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime}+\mathbf{a}^{\prime}=\mathbf{a}}|\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a},L}([\eta],\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{a}^{\prime})|

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime}+\mathbf{a}^{\prime}=\mathbf{a},(\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime},N}\times\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}^{\prime},M}}\big|\phi^{-1}_{[\eta]}(\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime})\big|

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime}+\mathbf{a}^{\prime}=\mathbf{a},(\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime})\in\phi_{[\eta]}(\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a},L}([\eta],\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{a}^{\prime}))}q^{k(\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime})}.

3.5
Weight

Based on the calculation of
\delta_{L}
, we introduce the notion of weight functions and weighted quantum cluster functions. Denote
\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}:=\bigcup_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{m}
.

We define

\operatorname{MF}:=\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}\times\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}=\{(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime})|\mathbf{c}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{m,M},\,\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{m,N},\,M,N\in\mathcal{A},\,m\in\mathbb{N}\}

and set

\mathbb{Z}_{\operatorname{MF}}:=\big\{f\colon\operatorname{MF}\times\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}\rightarrow Z|f(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},[\epsilon])=0\text{ unless }\mathbf{c}^{\prime}_{0}={\rm qt}\epsilon,\,\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}_{0}={\rm st}\epsilon\big\},

where
\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}=\coprod_{M,N\in\mathcal{A}}\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}(M,N)
and
Z=\big\{\frac{n}{2}\mid n\in\mathbb{Z}\big\}
is the set of all half integers. The functions in
\mathbb{Z}_{\operatorname{MF}}
are called weight functions.
Given
[\epsilon]\in\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}(M,N)
, we define

\mathbb{Z}_{\operatorname{MF}}[\epsilon]:=\{f\in\mathbb{Z}_{\operatorname{MF}}\mid f(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},[\rho])=0\text{ if }[\rho]\neq[\epsilon]\}.

Given
f\in\mathbb{Z}_{\operatorname{MF}}[\epsilon]
, we write
f(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},[\epsilon])
instead as
f(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime})
.

Definition 3.9

(weighted quantum cluster function)
.

Given a weight function
f\in\mathbb{Z}_{\operatorname{MF}}[\epsilon]
, the weighted quantum cluster function
f*_{[\epsilon]}\delta_{L}
is the linear function on
\mathcal{M}_{q}
defined by

\displaystyle f*_{[\epsilon]}\delta_{L}(d_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}})

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}+\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime}=\mathbf{a},(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime})\in\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}^{\prime},M}\times\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime},N}}\big|\phi^{-1}_{[\epsilon]}(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime})\big|\cdot q^{f(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime})}

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}+\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime}=\mathbf{a},(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime})\in\phi_{[\epsilon]}(\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a},L}([\epsilon],\mathbf{a}^{\prime},\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime}))}q^{k(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime})}\cdot q^{f(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime})},

where
M={\rm qt}\epsilon
,
N={\rm st}\epsilon
and
L={\rm mt}\epsilon
.

If
f\in\mathbb{Z}_{\operatorname{MF}}
is the zero function, we have
f*_{[\epsilon]}\delta_{L}=\delta_{L}
.
So weight functions provide
q
-deformations of
\delta_{L}
.

3.6
Multiplication

We now introduce the multiplication of weighted quantum cluster functions.

Definition 3.10

(multiplication of quantum cluster functions)
.

Given
M
,
N
in
\mathcal{A}
, we define the multiplication of quantum cluster functions as

\delta_{M}*\delta_{N}=\delta_{M\oplus N}.

By definition, we have

Proposition 3.11
.

The multiplication
*
is associative and commutative.

We denote the zero element in
\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}(M,N)
by
\mathbf{0}_{MN}
.

Remark 3.12
.

From the refinement of the quantum cluster function, we have

\delta_{M\oplus N}(d_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}})={\hskip-5.69046pt}\int_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}+\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime}=\mathbf{a}}|\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a},M\oplus N}(\mathbf{0}_{MN},\mathbf{a}^{\prime},\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime})|={\hskip-5.69046pt}\iint_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}+\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime}=\mathbf{a},(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime})\in\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}^{\prime},M}\times\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime},N}}q^{k(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime})}.

Notice that the image of
\phi_{\mathbf{0}_{MN}}
is the whole of
\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}^{\prime},M}\times\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime},N}
since any two chains
\mathbf{c}^{\prime}
,
\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}
can be assembled into a chain ending with
M\oplus N
through direct sum.

On the other hand, the convolution of the two functions
\delta_{M}
and
\delta_{N}
can be formally defined as

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}+\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime}=\mathbf{a}}\delta_{M}(d_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}^{\prime}})\cdot\delta_{N}(d_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime}})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}+\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime}=\mathbf{a}}|\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}^{\prime},M}|\cdot|\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime},N}|

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}+\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime}=\mathbf{a},(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime})\in\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}^{\prime},M}\times\mathcal{F}^{\rm mono}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime},N}}1.

So the definition of multiplication is a
q
-deformation of conventional convolution.

Definition 3.13

(multiplication of weighted quantum cluster functions)
.

Given weighted quantum cluster functions
f*_{[\epsilon^{\prime}]}\delta_{M}
and
g*_{[\epsilon^{\prime\prime}]}\delta_{N}
, we define the multiplication as

(f*_{[\epsilon^{\prime}]}\delta_{M})*(g*_{[\epsilon^{\prime\prime}]}\delta_{N})=h*_{\mathbf{0}_{MN}}\delta_{M\oplus N},

where
h(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime},[\epsilon])=0
unless
[\epsilon]={\mathbf{0}_{MN}}
and
h(\mathbf{c}^{\prime},\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime})=f(\phi_{[\epsilon^{\prime}]}(\mathbf{c}^{\prime}))+g(\phi_{[\epsilon^{\prime\prime}]}(\mathbf{c}^{\prime\prime}))
.

Proposition 3.14
.

The multiplication operation from Definition
3.13
is associative.

Proof.

Given
f_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\operatorname{MF}}[{\epsilon_{i}}]
such that
\operatorname{mt}\epsilon_{i}=M_{i}
for
i=1,2,3
, we set

\displaystyle((f_{1}*_{[\epsilon_{1}]}\delta_{M_{1}})*(f_{2}*_{[\epsilon_{2}]}\delta_{M_{2}}))*(f_{3}*_{[\epsilon_{3}]}\delta_{M_{3}})=f_{(12)3}*_{\mathbf{0}_{M_{1}\oplus M_{2},M3}}\delta_{M_{1}\oplus M_{2}\oplus M_{3}},

\displaystyle(f_{1}*_{[\epsilon_{1}]}\delta_{M_{1}})*((f_{2}*_{[\epsilon_{2}]}\delta_{M_{2}})*(f_{3}*_{[\epsilon_{3}]}\delta_{M_{3}}))=f_{1(23)}*_{\mathbf{0}_{M_{1},M_{2}\oplus M3}}\delta_{M_{1}\oplus M_{2}\oplus M_{3}},

where
f_{(12)3}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\operatorname{MF}}\mathbf{0}_{M_{1}\oplus M_{2},M3}
,
f_{1(23)}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\operatorname{MF}}\mathbf{0}_{M_{1},M_{2}\oplus M3}
.

Note that the non-weighted parts of the two compositions are the same.
We have

(f_{1}*_{[\epsilon_{1}]}\delta_{M_{1}})*(f_{2}*_{[\epsilon_{2}]}\delta_{M_{2}})=f_{(12)}*_{\mathbf{0}_{M_{1}\oplus M_{2}}}\delta_{M_{1}\oplus M_{2}},

where
